  My review of the dirty diff — one real finding beyond the quality items

  worker.rs:1259 (worker_request.size_mode = request.size_mode()) is dead at 
  that call site. run_selected_compile_worker unconditionally sets
  worker_request.selected = Some(selected) (line 1260), and the child dispatches
  at line 721–723: when selected is Some, only compile_selected_from_request
  runs — and that path reads selected.size_mode (line 613, already committed)
  not the top-level field. The generic route's reader is
  compile_grammar_from_request (line 459), which the selected route never
  reaches. So the mode genuinely crosses the boundary via
  SelectedCompileRequest.size_mode → from_worker_wire → emit.rs:4194; the new
  line adds a second, unread carrier. pack.rs:677 sets the same field for the
  generic health-compile route, and that one is live.

  I'll drop line 1259 rather than add a consistency check — the envelope-match
  check at 596–599 already fails closed, and one carrier per route is the honest
  shape.

  Quality items I've specced for a fix agent (all duplication, no behavior
  change): a ComposeEnvelope → ComposeBudget conversion to replace the copy in
  analyzer.rs and the identical inline closure in worker.rs; collapsing
  new_with_budget_and_profile_policy{,_and_compose} via Option<ComposeEnvelope>
  (~35 lines, including a duplicated unordered-strata check); collapsing the two
  *_for_mode constructors; folding build_pack_with_size_mode back into
  build_pack (only 4 call sites, 2 of them tests).

  Also confirmed: emit_with_budget_profiled_with_compose is
  argument-for-argument identical to the existing wrapper except
  selected_compose: Some(..) vs None, so it's behavior-preserving; and
  with_chain_depth_cap clamps, so DeveloperStress's CHAIN_DEPTH_ABSOLUTE_CEILING
  really does stay bounded.

  One thing to flag now: pg.ps1 passes no --features, so every
  developer-tools-gated test — including the new
  remove_size_limits_requires_the_foma_engine_for_parse_and_batch — is inert in 
  an ordinary -Mode test run. The plan doc says to rerun with --features 
  developer-tools as a second pass. That's a manual step nothing enforces, so
  the developer half of this contract can silently stop being tested. I'd like
  to fix that properly rather than rely on remembering; I'll come back to it
  once the agents report.

  Waiting on: test/failure evidence + the templated-selection fact, the
  Amharic/Aweti acceptance-gate plan, and the rebase conflict measurement.

● I need to correct something I told you earlier, and it changes item 5's
  verdict.

  I was wrong that the corpus blocker wasn't real. samples/data contains only
  the *-words.txt and *.fwdata files. Every grammar input the manifest marks
  required=True is absent:

  ┌────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────┐
  │  Language  │ Required grammar  │ Present? │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ indonesian │ indonesian-hc.xml │ missing  │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ sena       │ sena-hc.xml       │ missing  │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ amharic    │ amharic-hc.xml    │ missing  │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ aweti      │ aweti.json        │ missing  │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ mbugwe     │ mbugwe.fwdata     │ present  │
  └────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────┘

  Only mbugwe's required pair is complete. So the Indonesian 120-case gate
  cannot run here — and it can't be repointed at indonesian.fwdata either,
  because its case lock pins a byte-exact grammarSha256 of indonesian-hc.xml (I
  verified the gate asserts those hashes before using the file). I removed the
  junction and will use PANGLOSS_CORPUS_ROOT, which is the mechanism pg.ps1:229
  actually documents.

  So item 5 has two independent blockers, and neither is something I can code 
  around:

  1. Missing private grammar inputs — verified above. Nothing in the repo can
     synthesize an -hc.xml from a .fwdata that matches a pinned hash.
  2. Compiler gaps, verified in source, not taken on the agent's word:
     - capability.rs's templated_shape_floor returns a refusal for any
       Role::Infix allomorph — reason string: "the templated proposer has no 
       Copy-Insert-Copy/infix entry". Amharic's morphology is root-and-pattern
       interdigitation, so this is categorical, not a budget limit.
     - five_language_backend_reports_gate.rs's assert_default_resource_no_path
       asserts is_no_path(), preferred() == None, and selected().is_empty() —
       and Amharic, Aweti, and Mbugwe all call it. Under the default envelope
       those grammars have no admitted backend at all, so there is no route to
       certify.

  The honest reachable outcome for item 5 is therefore not 3/3, and not even 1/3
  from this worktree — it's 0/3 certified here, with the Indonesian gate 
  written and believed-correct but unrunnable for want of indonesian-hc.xml, 
  plus two backend gaps that are their own compiler projects (Aweti needs 6
  named recall misses closed and selection wiring; Amharic needs an infix
  emission mechanism that doesn't exist). I'm not going to write gates for
  Amharic and Aweti that can't run and can't pass — that would manufacture the
  appearance of coverage. I'll document the blockers precisely instead, which is
  what makes the status honest.

  Rebase is settled and low-risk: the probe replayed all 117 commits with
  exactly one mechanical conflict — a duplicate mod backend_cards_data;
  insertion in pg-foma/src/lib.rs, because main alphabetized it in the interim.
  Resolution is to drop the duplicate line and keep pub mod completed_build;. My
  main.rs conflict prediction didn't materialize for committed history, but it
  still may when my new commit replays, since main and I both edit run_batch.

  Still waiting on the verification agent (test evidence + the
  templated-selection fact) before I commit. Nothing else can proceed until I
  know whether that gate repair is sound.

      43  The escape hatch is `PANGLOSS_ALLOW_BARE_CARGO=1`, deliberately
           an env var — needing it means the
      44  managed path is broken and should be fixed, not routed around.
      45  
      46 +**A feature-gated test needs a second pass, and a one-pass run 
         +cannot tell you it was skipped.**                              
      47 +`pg.ps1` passes no `--features`, so every `#[cfg(feature = "dev
         +eloper-tools")]` test is *compiled                             
      48 +out* of an ordinary `-Mode test` run. It does not appear as a f
         +ailure, and it does not appear as a                            
      49 +skip either — it appears as nothing at all, which is exactly th
         +e shape of "I could not look" reading                          
      50 +as "everything is fine" that this file refuses elsewhere. Measu
         +red: `pg-cli --test                                            
      51 +developer_flags_contract` runs **2** tests by default and **4**
         + with the feature on, and the two                              
      52 +sets are disjoint — the production tests assert the flags are r
         +ejected as unknown options, the                                
      53 +developer tests assert they are honoured. Verifying only the de
         +fault half proves nothing about the                            
      54 +other.                                                         
      55 +                                                               
      56 +So any change touching a developer-gated flag, API, or `Compile
         +SizeMode` variant must be verified                             
      57 +**twice**. `pg.ps1` reads `PANGLOSS_EXTRA_ARGS` (a binder-proof
         + passthrough appended after                                    
      58 +`-ExtraArgs`), so the second pass is:                          
      59 +```powershell                                                  
      60 +$env:PANGLOSS_EXTRA_ARGS = '--features developer-tools'        
      61 +& .\rust\tools\pg.ps1 -Mode test -Package pg-cli -TestTarget de
         +veloper_flags_contract                                         
      62 +$env:PANGLOSS_EXTRA_ARGS = ''                                  
      63 +```                                                            
      64 +Read the printed cargo line back and confirm it really contains
         + `--features developer-tools`; a pass                          
      65 +that silently lost the flag is indistinguishable from a green r
         +un of the wrong half. Clearing the                             
      66 +variable afterward matters — left set, it silently re-features 
         +every later build in the session.                              
      67 +

  On the test repair. The synthetic selection turned out to be necessary, not a
  shortcut: BACKEND_PREFERENCE is a fixed tie-break with TunedSurfaceProbed
  first, and findings are only ever attached to the tuned report — so whenever
  tuned is accepted cleanly it ties with templated and wins. Across 59 assessed
  fixtures, no grammar in this repo ever gives TemplatedUnderlyingTokens the 
  preferred() seat. What was wrong was the claim: the comment said ranked
  reports drove selection while the assertions were tautological. Each case now
  passes its own selection, so the provenance is visible at the call site.

  with an allow_incomplete: bool branch. It was right and I was wrong —

  feature. I've told it to keep the closure.

  Beyond the handoff: the dedup refactor (−72 lines, verified), the manifest
  declaration fix, the CLAUDE.md two-pass rule, and a corrected status record.

  The single most useful thing I learned, now written into the doc: every green
  result on this branch — including the handoff's — came from narrow -TestTarget
  runs. Those compile only the named target, so they never built the broken
  gate and never ran the failing pg-cli tests. A targeted run is a claim about
  one target, never about a package. That's the same hazard shape as the repo's
  own -Scope rule and the developer-tools two-pass rule: a narrower run
  reporting identically to a broader one.

  Waiting on the retried Fable review for the marker-gate disposition and
  GO/NO-GO. I won't push or rebase until that lands.

● The Fable review came back NO-GO, and it was right on every claim I checked.
  Here's what it found and what I've done.

  Verified independently, all three confirmed:

  - The marker gate is absent from main — branch-local. Merging would have put a
    non-compiling pg-foma test suite into the integration line.
  - Both failing test files are byte-identical on main (413b5406, 26fb47ba) and
    the short-circuit exists on main. main's pg-cli suite is red right now. Item
    4 is main's defect, not this branch's — which also means the short-circuit
    merged to main without a full-suite run. Same narrow-run disease, one level
    up. You should know that regardless of what happens to this branch.
  - Commit 1514dc92 exists only on my branch, not on the live
    circumfix-cross-product worktree that owns the openspec change.

  Actions taken:

  - Removed the marker gate (34dace6c), naming 1514dc92 in the commit body so
    recovery is one command. I want to be plain that this deletes another
    workstream's tracked test from this branch — the reviewer's argument
    persuaded me: a red gate that fails to compile rather than to fail is an
    authoring defect, and the proof is that task 1.3's checkbox says "verify RED
    with -TestTarget templated_morphology_marker_gate", a command that could
    only ever have shown compile errors. The checkbox was ticked on a state that
    doesn't satisfy it.
  - Fixed all four P0 doc errors. Two were false when I committed them: the doc
    claimed the Indonesian gate wasn't declared in the manifest when my own
    commit 50 minutes earlier had declared it, and it claimed the regressions
    were "invisible without the corpus" when they reproduce on a synthetic
    fixture with no corpus at all.
  - Closed two P2s: documented the size_mode wire field's dual-carrier
    behaviour, and removed the stray target-tdd/.

  Two narration errors of mine it caught, both real: I attributed the dead-line
  deletion to 849c2eb9 when 849c2eb9 added it and 9a1ee996 removed it; and I
  described eca66881 to the reviewer as a hygiene-gate section when it actually
  documents feature-gate invisibility. The underlying facts held up — it
  verified the dead line is genuinely unread, and confirmed the refactor
  argument-for-argument — but I described my own commits carelessly in a handoff
  chain, which is exactly how the next person inherits a wrong picture.

  On item 4, the reviewer overruled my instinct and I accept it. I'd thought
  repointing the fixture might be a cheap non-architectural fix. It isn't: the
  test file's header says the fixture was chosen because one unbounded run
  produced the exact mix needed, citing a measured evaluation order. The
  fixtures didn't become unsuitable — the compiler stopped measuring them.
  Repointing would convert "the optimizer measures marker-bearing strata
  grammars" into "the optimizer measures some grammar," which is the
  coverage-inheritance trap this repo has already been burned by. So the
  handoff's "fix the four regressions but avoid architectural expansion" is
  self-contradictory as applied here, and item 4 belongs with the circumfix
  change where task 3.2 is already specified. I'm handing it back rather than
  hacking it.

  Item 5 it judged the right call, not under-delivery.

  Running the first genuine full-package verification this branch has ever had —
  pg-foma both feature configs, pg-cli with --no-fail-fast so I see every
  failure rather than the first four. Rebase and push are gated on it.

● Chasing my own overclaim turned into the most useful thing I did in this
  stretch.

  The correction

  I had asserted — including in a committed document — that every
  manifest-required grammar input was absent from this machine. That was based
  on listing one directory. A scoped search found three of the four sitting in
  other worktrees: amharic-hc.xml and sena-hc.xml in hc-stats, aweti.json in an
  agent worktree. Assembled with the word lists, 9 of 10 required inputs are 
  present. The corpus was one file short, not four.

  What that bought — real acceptance evidence

  With those grammars under PANGLOSS_CORPUS_ROOT:

  PASS  amharic_backend_reports_are_complete  (1.047s)   real amharic-hc.xml
  PASS  aweti_backend_reports_are_complete    (1.490s)   real aweti.json

  Both assert is_no_path(), preferred() == None, selected().is_empty(). Passing
  against real language data is positive measured evidence that Amharic and
  Aweti have no admitted backend — replacing what had been my inference from
  reading capability.rs. That's the part of item 5 that was actually achievable,
  and it's now done on evidence rather than argument.

  Indonesian failed, and failed correctly: corpus.rs:91 refused with "This test 
  was requested explicitly, so it fails rather than reporting a pass it did not 
  earn." Exactly the behaviour you want from a missing input. I ran this as a
  targeted measurement, not -Mode corpus-test — that mode rightly refuses while
  indonesian-hc.xml is absent, and I've said so in the doc rather than claiming
  its guarantees.

  Final state

  origin/fst/selected-payload-gate-rebased = 29b61c5f = local HEAD. Tree clean,
  0 behind main, 129 ahead, hygiene clean. Eleven commits.

  indonesian-hc.xml is now the single highest-leverage missing artifact in this 
  whole picture. One file unblocks the 120-case selected-payload gate, the four
  skipped net-queryable tests, and the only route that could move certification
  off 0/3. It must be the exact bytes the case lock's grammarSha256 pins —
  another copy of "Indonesian" won't do. If you know where it lives, that's the
  next move.

  Two things still need you:

  1. The force-push, if you want the original branch ref moved — gated, verified
     safe (only one commit differs by patch-id, and both versions add
     byte-identical content):

● Pushing past my own overclaim produced the most valuable result of the
  session, and it settles the question item 4 was stuck on.

  The real-corpus run resolved the open design question

  With the corpus complete, corpus_indonesian_confirms_after_the_finish_step ran
  for the first time on the real Indonesian grammar and failed with seven
  certifications:

  - five Unsupported { "plan requires subtrees build_controllable does not build
    (CompositeEmissionMarker, StructuralCompositeMarker)" }
  - one StaticRejected — TunedSurface needs >3,000 rule pairs, stopped at 3,001:
    "the current TunedSurface operational envelope is too small"
  - one BuildFailed — templated emission Partial { uncovered: 3 }

  That is the same short-circuit at backend_runtime.rs:1830-1838 behind item 4's
  four pg-cli failures — reproducing on the reference grammar, not a staged 
  fixture. So the hypothesis I'd been hoping for, that those failures were a
  fixture-selection defect fixable by repointing, is dead: no choice of fixture
  avoids a refusal that fires on the reference grammar. The Fable reviewer's
  verdict is now confirmed by measurement rather than argument, and my instinct
  was wrong for a concrete reason.

  The test's own message even records it once passed — "Pre-fix this read 0 of 3
  confirmed with a merasa multiplicity mismatch" — so marker-bearing candidates
  were measurable once and aren't now.

  Net results from completing the corpus

  Gate: five_language_backend_reports_gate
  Result: 5/5 PASS under -Mode corpus-test, 5 recorded cases, all five real
  grammars
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gate: backend_runtime_net_is_queryable_gate
  Result: 3 of 4 corpus tests PASS, 9 recorded cases
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gate: indonesian_worker_selected_payload_gate
  Result: FAIL — EnumerationBudgetReached at 1,016 pairs, while the named retry
  raises only the closure-work cap to 10,000

  The certification gate's failure is a precisely located envelope-dimension
  mismatch: the retry lifts the wrong limit. Indonesian is still not certified,
  but "a file is missing" has become an actionable one-line diagnosis.

  clean, hygiene clean.

  The certification gate dies on EnumerationBudgetReached, and no shipped

  usize::MAX. So I can ask the question directly: does the Indonesian grammar

  That's not a fix and I won't present it as one — a developer-stress compile
  can't certify anything, by design. But the answer changes what you'd decide:

  - If it compiles — the blocker is purely "how large a budget," and the

● That last dig was worth it — verifying my own claim turned a decision request
  into a concrete defect.

  New finding: a retry is authorized for a limit no envelope raises

  emit.rs:4413 trips EnumerationBudgetReached from enum_budget.trip_reason(), so
  it is the enumeration dimension — my doc was right. But
  resource_envelope.rs:464-472 lists that stop reason as retryable, alongside
  WorkBudgetReached and DepthBudgetReached. And the only envelope a retry can
  move to raises tuned_surface_closure_work_cap and nothing else — both
  envelopes carry identical enumeration caps.

  So a compile that dies this way is handed a retry authorization that can only 
  re-enter the same limit and fail identically. The trip site says as much from
  the other side: "a floor, not a total (limit {limit}; Aweti's measured 
  uncapped total is ~15x this cap)" — a cap routinely exceeded by an order of
  magnitude, declared retryable, with nowhere to retry into.

  That reframes item 5's remainder from "pick a budget number" to a choice
  between opposites:

  1. Add an envelope raising the enumeration caps — makes the existing
     authorization mean something, and is the path that could certify
     Indonesian.
  2. Remove EnumerationBudgetReached from the retryable set — stop promising a
     recovery the engine can't perform.

  Doing neither leaves a retry path guaranteed to fail, which is the worst
  option. That's a real defect in shipped behaviour, not a missing feature, and
  it's now on the record with both resolutions.

  Final state

  origin/fst/selected-payload-gate-rebased = ed3f6008, thirteen commits, tree
  clean, 0 behind main / 132 ahead, hygiene clean.

  Items 1, 2, 3, 6 done. Item 7 verified and delivered to a ref; moving the
  original needs the gated force-push. Items 4 and 5 are each blocked on one

  - Item 4 — replace strategy_coverage.rs's ProcessMorphology => CannotRepresent
    row with a predicate-backed disposition (circumfix change, task 3.2).
    Proven necessary on the real Indonesian grammar, not just a fixture.
  - Item 5 — the retry/envelope inconsistency above.

  I've stopped short of implementing either, and I want to be plain about why
  rather than restate it again: both change shipped, wire-serialized behaviour,
  one belongs to another workstream, and the handoff explicitly ruled out
  architectural expansion. Doing them anyway would hand you work that looks
  finished and carries a compatibility decision nobody made. The useful thing I
  could do was reduce them from open questions to named choices, and that's
  done.

● That last check was worth doing — it corrected my own finding in the direction
  of less alarm, which is the direction I'm least likely to drift toward under
  pressure to show completion.

  What I had written was imprecise. I'd said a failing compile "re-enters the
  same limit and fails identically." Actually CompileEnvelopeRequest::retry_from
  refuses a same-envelope retry — pinned by closure_terminal_parity_gate.rs:230
  — and requires escalating to a different envelope id. The design is coherent:
  authorize, then escalate. The real gap is narrower: for this stop reason the
  only escalation target raises tuned_surface_closure_work_cap and nothing else,
  so escalation is permitted but futile.

  And two qualifications I'd omitted: no production code reads 
  retry_authorization() at all — the only callers are tests — so nothing shipped
  performs a futile retry today; the gap is latent. And the authorization
  itself is correct: the failure genuinely is the retryable kind. What's missing
  is an envelope worth retrying into.

  That matters practically: it means option 2 (declare it terminal) only removes
  a misleading affordance, while option 1 (add an envelope) is the only path to
  certification. And the choice is still free precisely because nothing depends
  on it yet.

  live circumfix-cross-product worktree.

  handoff's own "Closure suite: 7/7, Worker supervisor: 5/5" — came from narrow
  -TestTarget runs. Those compile only the named target, so they never built the
  broken gate and never ran the failing tests. That's now written into
  CLAUDE.md.

  to this branch. So the marker short-circuit merged to main without a
  full-package run.

  in-repo indonesian-words.txt is the wrong 750-byte file and fails the lock's

● That reclassification changes one of my earlier conclusions, and mostly for
  the better. Here's how it lands against measured data.

  Your rule vs. what the code does today

  Your split — size limits are advisory/overridable, "can't represent it" is a 
  hard no — is already the distinction this repo says it wants (the worktree
  CLAUDE.md opens with "Classify FST evidence before changing limits…
  correctness/representability vs production readiness vs resource
  containment"). The problem is the code doesn't honour it: resource caps 
  currently produce the same hard no-path as representability gaps. That's why
  all five grammars report "no path" and you can't tell which are
  unrepresentable and which just wanted a bigger number.

  The five grammars, classified your way

  All measured today against real data (the five-language gate now runs — I
  found the grammars in other worktrees):

  So: one of five fits today. Three are blocked only by numbers. One (Amharic) 
  has a genuine representability wall — and separately a resource question
  behind it.

  Your three questions

  1 — Item 4 (the four recipe regressions). Your rule changes my answer. I'd
  concluded it needed the circumfix change's task 3.2 (predicate-backed
  ProcessMorphology). Looking again at the seven certifications on the real
  Indonesian grammar: five are the PlanComposed marker refusal (correct, stays),
  one is Templated Partial (representability, stays) — and one is TunedSurface 
  StaticRejected for being 1 visit over a 3,000 cap. Under your rule that
  becomes a warning, TunedSurface gets to run, and confirmed > 0 becomes
  reachable. That's a much smaller change than task 3.2, and it doesn't touch
  another workstream. I'd want to verify TunedSurface actually confirms once
  allowed to proceed — that's measurable and currently unmeasured — but the path
  is real.

  2 — Item 5 (the envelope). Your rule kills the option I was leaning toward.
  Don't add a third ResourceEnvelopeId inching the cap up; that's wire-format
  churn chasing a number that'll be wrong for the next grammar. Make the caps
  overridable in place, and let the real ceiling be the machine — procgov's
  job-object cap and the RSS watchdog, which already exist and already give you
  a truthful "it crashed at 30GB." Nicely, this dissolves the defect I found:
  EnumerationBudgetReached is declared retryable with nowhere to retry to — with
  overridable caps the retry target is the same envelope with the limit lifted,
  so no new envelope id and no wire change at all.

  3 — The force-push is unaffected by any of this; it's still just a permission
  gate. git push --force-with-lease origin 
  HEAD:refs/heads/fst/selected-payload-gate.

  What this makes buildable

  Most of the machinery exists. --remove-size-limits already sets enumeration
  caps to usize::MAX while keeping CHAIN_DEPTH_ABSOLUTE_CEILING and the external
  watchdog — it's exactly your "easy to override." Two gaps: it's
  developer-tools-gated so it doesn't exist in a shipped build, and it's
  all-or-nothing rather than per-dimension.

  The concrete work is roughly: split the refusal reasons into resource-class
  and representability-class at the point they're produced; make resource-class
  emit a warning plus a proceed-anyway path instead of StaticRejected/no-path;
  keep representability-class exactly as it is. Then re-run all five and get the
  "fits / crashes" answer you actually want.

  Want me to scope that properly as its own piece of work? It's a behaviour
  change to shipped refusals, so I'd want a plan and a Fable review on the
  classification boundary before writing code — the one thing I'd guard hard is
  that "resource" never quietly swallows a representability gap, since that's
  how you'd get a silently incomplete FST.

  already lifts the caps and pg.ps1 -Mode run already contains the blast radius

● Agent(Measure five grammars uncapped) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Acting on your decision rather than asking again.

  A Sonnet agent is running the sweep now: builds a developer-tools pangloss,
  then attempts each of the five grammars twice — managed, and with
  --remove-size-limits — every attempt wrapped in a 30 GB kernel job object via
  pg.ps1 -Mode run -RunMemoryGB 30. So "crashes your computer at 30GB" becomes a
  recorded outcome instead of an actual crash: the process fails its own
  allocation and the machine stays up. Sequential, never two uncapped attempts
  at once.

  It reports each row as one of FITS / REFUSED-RESOURCE /
  REFUSED-REPRESENTABILITY / CRASHED-30GB, and for every refusal it must say
  which of your two classes it is — quoting the text — since that split is the
  whole point.

  Why measure before changing the classification: right now I can tell you
  Indonesian is refused for being one visit over a 3,000 cap, and Amharic is
  refused for Role::Infix — but I can't tell you whether Amharic's
  3M-and-still-growing closure terminates at all with caps off, or whether
  Aweti's 15× enumeration overflow is 3 GB or 300 GB. Those determine whether
  "lift the cap" is a real answer per grammar or just moves the failure. After
  this sweep the reclassification is a mechanical edit against known outcomes
  rather than a guess.

  What I expect to be able to hand you: Sena fits today. Indonesian, Mbugwe and
  Aweti are each blocked only by numbers, so they're the candidates for "fits"
  once caps are advisory. Amharic is the interesting one — it has both a hard
  representability wall (infix, which no memory fixes) and a resource question
  behind it, so it may end up "critical no-go" regardless.

  One thing I'll hold firm on when the classification change comes:
  resource-class must never quietly absorb a representability gap. Amharic's
  infix refusal has to stay a hard no even after every cap becomes advisory —
  otherwise you get an FST that builds cleanly and silently can't parse the
  morphology it was built for. That's the one failure mode worth more than the
  caps.

● Grammar sweep stopped; that work is parked until this lands.

  The design doc is docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-23-stress-grammar-constructio
  n-and-production-admission.md, and its load-bearing sentence is exactly your
  point:

  ▎ The compiler answers three independent questions — 
  ▎ correctness/representability, production readiness, resource containment. 
  ▎ "No severity, flag, or resource result may blur those questions into one 
  ▎ admission decision."

  Your Warning/Error/Critical split is already written there:

  - Warning — cleanup or cost risk; a complete proven artifact may still be
    production-ready
  - Error — not production-ready under normal policy, but the backend may have a
    complete, exact strategy and may be attempted in stress mode
  - Critical — cannot prove a recall-preserving representation; production 
    cannot override
  - A resource termination says only that this attempt didn't finish inside
    containment — never that the language is unsupported

  So the vocabulary exists and is good. What hasn't happened is the code sweep —
  152 severity assignments across 11 files, concentrated in health_evaluator.rs
  (42), health.rs (41), pack.rs (24), characterization.rs (15).

  I have concrete evidence the blur is real: on the actual Indonesian grammar,
  the tuned backend is refused for being one visit over a 3,000 cap ("the
  current TunedSurface operational envelope is too small") — pure resource — and
  that produces the same is_no_path() as a genuine BackendCoverageIncomplete.
  Indistinguishable downstream, which is precisely what the spec forbids.

  Three read-only audits are running in parallel against one shared rubric, so
  the fix list is derived rather than guessed:

  1. Severity inventory — every assignment in
     health.rs/health_evaluator.rs/characterization.rs, each verdicted CORRECT /
     TOO-HARSH (resource treated as Critical) / TOO-LENIENT / BLURRED.
  2. Admission funnel — how a finding becomes CompileDecision → BackendSelection
     → published pack; where resource and representability converge; what
     --remove-size-limits and --allow-unproven can actually get past today.
  3. Doc checklist — every normative statement turned into a numbered testable
     assertion (that becomes the unit-test plan), plus any doc-vs-doc
     contradictions.

  I asked audit 1 to put TOO-LENIENT findings first, because that's the
  dangerous direction: a representability gap softened to Warning/Error yields a
  grammar that builds a clean FST and silently can't represent its own
  morphology. Relaxing caps is recoverable; that isn't.

  One tension I'll need your call on once the audits land: the spec says Error
  results are never published as production artifacts, while you want Errors
  easy to override. Those reconcile if an overridden Error yields a
  developer-stamped artifact rather than a production-publishable one — which is
  what --remove-size-limits already means. I'll come back with the specific
  boundary rather than assume it, and I'd like a Fable review on that boundary
  before code changes, since it's where a mistake would silently ship unproven
  output.

● Found the architectural crux. HealthReport::admission():

  pub fn admission(&self) -> Severity {
      self.findings.iter().map(|f| f.severity).max().unwrap_or(Severity::Ideal)
  }

  A max() over every finding — three independent questions collapsed into one 
  severity. That is literally the thing the spec forbids. A caller seeing Error
  cannot tell "too big to ship" from "partially unrepresentable." Everything
  downstream inherits that ambiguity.

  The keystone fix is a classification function, so the rule becomes
  machine-checked rather than convention. Dispatching that now — it's purely
  additive, so it can't conflict with the other workstreams:

● Three agents running (2 audits + 1 implementation), one slot held in reserve.

  What the first audit established

  One dangerous inversion, already verified: FomaTier::Unsupported — backend
  produced no usable network at all — is rated Error under
  UnknownUnboundedConstruct, while FomaTier::Partial (only some candidates
  omitted) is Critical. The strictly worse condition carries the weaker
  severity, and the test pinning it is named ..._unsupported_tier_is_critical
  while asserting Severity::Error. A test whose name records the intent its body
  violates.

  One systemic cause of over-harshness: Severity::overridable() returns false
  for every variant, unconditionally. There is no override surface for resource
  findings at all — so your "easy to override" doesn't exist yet, anywhere.

  One flagship inconsistency: the same fact — ordering-rule count over
  DEFAULT_ORDERING_MULTIPLICITY_BUDGET, same metric, same constant — is Critical
  at characterization.rs:632 and Error at health_evaluator.rs:580. Which
  severity you get depends only on which phase noticed first. That's a numeric
  cap producing an unoverridable dead end.

  Roughly a dozen more resource-class caps (enumeration budget, net size,
  alpha-tuple, group, emit-line, compose timeout, chain depth, compound-pair,
  apply-time) are all unoverridable Error. One of them, the apply-budget
  finding, already says in its own explanation text that "the word is
  incomplete, never a definitive partial analysis… may be explicitly resubmitted
  with a larger apply budget" — the rubric's own language, attached to a
  finding nothing can override.

  The architectural fix

  admission() is where the blur happens, and I've dispatched its foundation:
  FindingClass { Representability, Readiness, Containment, Process } plus
  FindingCode::class() as an exhaustive match — so a future finding code cannot 
  compile until someone classifies it. Process is a deliberate fourth class: the
  audit found a catch-all lumping InternalConstructionFault (the compiler may
  be broken) together with four resource reasons, so a caller can't distinguish
  "declined for cost" from "the tool failed."

  I told that agent explicitly not to add a test tying class() to Severity yet —
  the tree currently violates that invariant at characterization.rs:632, and I
  don't want a red test sitting in the branch. That invariant test ships with
  the fix that makes it pass.

  Next, once the admission-funnel and doc audits land: split admission() into a
  per-question verdict, correct the inverted Unsupported severity, resolve the
  same-fact-two-severities split, and only then wire the override surface — with
  a Fable review on the boundary before that last one, since it's where a
  mistake silently publishes unproven output.

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                         │ that can't distinguish "too expensive" from   │
  │                         │ "the compiler may be broken"                  │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Queued behind the review — the four that carry real risk: splitting
  admission() per-axis, making validate_health_readiness class-aware, retyping
  the cost-ceiling Refuse, and threading envelopes into selection.

  The question I put to the reviewer

  The docs say headroom comes from "an explicit retry with a larger named 
  resource envelope" — not from --remove-size-limits. So when I thread envelope
  awareness into select_backends, it should probably take an envelope id only,
  never a CompileSizeMode — keeping the developer flag permanently out of the
  selection layer. I lean that way but asked it to argue the point, because
  getting it wrong is how a developer flag becomes a production escape hatch.

  The invariant I flagged as P0: no step may create a path that publishes an 
  Error/Critical artifact as production. Right now pack.rs merges capability
  findings into health findings and applies one undifferentiated severity >= 
  Error test across both origins — so making that class-aware is exactly where a
  mistake would leak.

  Two things worth knowing regardless

  The docs already encode your split precisely: resource-class Error cleared by
  choosing a bigger official envelope (ordinary operation, no flag);
  correctness-class Critical needs the hidden dev-only flag and is permanently
  stamped trust=unproven. Your instinct and the spec agree — the code just never
  implemented it.

  And a stale archived doc still asserts the old conflation in the present tense
  — "Error and Critical are BOTH overridable via the ADR 0005 capability 
  override" — which is the exact confusion this sweep exists to remove. That
  goes on the docs-alignment pass, along with retiring "No path" and "trusted
  FST" from the historical five-language tables, since "No path" reads as a
  verdict about the language when it only ever meant "no backend admitted under
  this envelope for this attempt."

  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to a3ad67d272c5fd03d at its next tool 
     round.

● The Fable review came back GO-WITH-CHANGES and caught two things that would
  have bitten hard.

  P0 — a publish-invariant breach I was walking into

  Step F (retyping the cost-ceiling Refuse) looked safe in isolation. Traced
  end-to-end, it isn't:

  1. Removing the Refuse makes capability_trust = Proven with no override record
  2. --remove-size-limits sets ordering_multiplicity_cap: None, so the
     over-budget grammar compiles
  3. pack never calls characterization_findings — so the ordering-stratum Error
     never reaches fst_health, and the backend report carries no finding either
     (only TunedSurface gets one attached)
  4. validate_health_readiness passes, a completeness certificate is issued, and
     the manifest records neither size_mode nor an envelope id

  Net: a stress-built, over-budget grammar publishes as clean/Proven/certified
  with zero Error. Today the Refuse forces --allow-unproven, whose
  trust=unproven stamp is indelible — F removes that stamp with nothing
  replacing it. So F goes last, and only after the manifest records envelope +
  size mode and the finding reaches fst_health on every path.

  The fix I had in flight was itself wrong

  Corrected mid-task, as above. Worth noting the shape: I made exactly the error
  this sweep exists to eliminate — collapsing two causes into one severity
  because they shared a code path.

  Also found, unaudited

  validate_health_readiness(&…, watchdog) passes the flag as worker_containment
  (pack.rs:360), so every --watchdog run reports "a worker containment failure"
  when nothing fired — a Process condition mislabelled as Containment. And
  project_overridden_capability_findings is origin-blind: it deletes every
  BackendCoverageIncomplete, including observed Partial-tier gaps the override
  never named, so the manifest under-reports what was overridden.

  Revised order

  A classification → B′ split-by-cause → C containment-never-Critical → NEW: 
  record-keeping (manifest carries envelope + size mode; findings reach
  fst_health on every path) → D per-axis verdict, additive → G envelope-id into
  selection → H new envelope rungs → E class-aware remedies → F last, atomically
  with its replacement blocker.

  On your "easy to override": the reviewer argues selection should take an
  envelope id only, never CompileSizeMode — since that enum is cfg-gated,
  threading it into selection would make candidacy differ between production and
  developer builds of the same library. So the override you want becomes a
  public --envelope <id> flag, and --remove-size-limits stays scoped to a single
  compile attempt. I agree, and it also means H needs an ordering-multiplicity 
  rung too — both shipped envelopes hardcode Some(100), so once F lands, that
  cap would otherwise have no route at all.

● The split surfaced exactly one test that had encoded the old flat behaviour,

  FomaError::Unsupported, which is a representability failure and is now

  justification has to hold up:

    backend error" — i.e. every error blocks. It asserts that still.
  - A uniform severity across all errors is precisely what the spec forbids ("no
    severity may blur those questions"). The old assertion encoded the defect.
  - No coverage is lost, it's relocated: the exact severity per cause is now
    pinned by ..._unbounded_rule_application_is_critical and
    ..._depth_budget_stop_is_containment_not_critical, both of which also assert
    the FindingClass.

  If I'd instead made Unsupported stay Error to keep the assertion, I'd have
  preserved the bug to preserve the test.

  rungs, class-aware remedies, cost-ceiling retype last, docs alignment. Call it
  a bit past halfway on code.

  On the naming — you're right, and it's not cosmetic

  Critical → Unsupported is more accurate, and I'd do it. The reason is sharper
  than readability.

  Severity is an ordered scale consumed by max(). A scale only makes sense
  within one axis. And the three axes aren't alike:

  - Readiness is genuinely graduated — Ideal → Info → Warning → Error. "Somewhat
    too big" is meaningful.
  - Representability is binary. You can prove a recall-preserving representation
    or you can't. There is no "slightly unrepresentable."
  - Containment is binary. The attempt finished inside its boundary or it
    didn't.

  Critical exists as the top band of a readiness scale only because 
  representability was crammed into that scale. The name is the blur, wearing a
  severity word.

  The practical payoff: renaming makes the error visible at the point of use.
  max(Warning, Critical) looks like a scale. max(Warning, Unsupported) looks
  obviously wrong — which is exactly the misuse we're hunting. It also matches
  the spec's own words ("cannot prove a recall-preserving representation") and
  FomaTier::Unsupported, which already uses the term for the same fact.

  On Error — I'd keep it. Your "maybe" is fair, but Warning/Error read naturally
  as one graduated readiness scale, and readiness is the axis where a scale
  belongs. Rename it and you'd want NotProductionReady, which is long and loses
  the pairing. Better: keep Error, and let Unsupported sitting beside it signal
  "this one is not the same kind of thing."

  Cost is small and known: 10 occurrences across 10 files, plus 4 files with the
  serialized "critical" literal. It's rename_all = "snake_case", so I'd add
  #[serde(alias = "critical")] so stored reports still parse, and bump
  HEALTH_SCHEMA_VERSION — the same pattern we just used for the manifest.

  One caveat worth your call: the truly correct end state is Critical leaving
  Severity altogether, with representability as a separate binary verdict rather
  than a band. The rename gets ~80% of the clarity for ~10% of the churn, and
  doesn't block that later. Want me to slot the rename in now — before the
  remaining steps write more Critical call sites — or take the full split?

  My recommendation

  NearLimit  →  OverLimit  →  CannotRepresent

  That triple does the work you're after. The first two describe a relationship 
  to a limit and are genuinely ordered — near, then over. The third describes
  something that isn't a limit at all. So the axis break becomes visible in the
  names themselves: max(NearLimit, OverLimit) is sensible, and max(OverLimit, 
  CannotRepresent) looks obviously wrong, which is precisely the misuse we've
  been hunting.

  For the remaining two, Ideal → WithinLimits and Info → Elevated would complete
  the scale coherently.

  Cost is unchanged from my earlier estimate: 10 Severity::Critical sites, 4
  files carrying the serialized literal, #[serde(alias = "critical")] for stored
  reports, one HEALTH_SCHEMA_VERSION bump.

  write more call sites under the old names.

❯ LargeMultiplier (Warning - check grammar optimization)                        

● Confirmed — the signals exist. And your four-way split passes the real test of
  a taxonomy: each level gets a different remedy.

  ┌─────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │      Level      │        The fact        │            Remedy            │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ LargeMultiplier │ A cost multiplier is   │ Check grammar optimization   │
  │                 │ large; nothing failed  │                              │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ OverLimit       │ An artificial cap      │ Retry under a larger named   │
  │                 │ stopped us             │ envelope                     │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ The machine could not  │ More RAM or a different      │
  │ MachineLimit    │ do it                  │ algorithm — no envelope      │
  │                 │                        │ helps                        │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ CannotRepresent │ The feature isn't      │ Implement it, or use the     │
  │                 │ built                  │ full engine                  │
  └─────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Four distinct remedies means four genuinely distinct facts. Today all four
  collapse into "no path," which is why you can't tell "it fits" from "it
  crashes at 30 GB" — that's the same complaint you opened with.

  One thing this is not: a rename

  MachineLimit has no producer today. The signals exist in WorkerOutcome, but
  they don't reach health findings as a distinct verdict — they land as
  Process/build failure. So this is new wiring, and two variants are genuinely
  ambiguous:

  - ChildCrashed — its own doc says "panic-as-abort, stack overflow, allocator 
    OOM, or an external kill." Allocator OOM is MachineLimit; a panic is
    Process. Same variant, two verdicts.
  - WallTimeoutKilled — doc says "an uninstrumented stall or too small an 
    envelope." Stall is Process/MachineLimit; too-small-envelope is OverLimit.

  Clean ones: RssLimitExceeded → MachineLimit; SpawnFailed/ProtocolViolation →
  Process.

  I'd rather tell you that than quietly pick a side and have MachineLimit mean
  two things. Options: split ChildCrashed by exit signature (OOM is
  distinguishable on Windows), or start conservative — only RssLimitExceeded is
  MachineLimit, everything ambiguous stays Process — and tighten later with
  evidence.

  Structural consequence

  Four named conditions can't sit on one ordered scale: MachineLimit
  (containment — this machine, this attempt) and CannotRepresent
  (representability — no machine helps) aren't comparable, so max() over them is
  still a blur. But we already built the answer: FindingClass carries the axis,
  Severity carries the level.

  WithinLimits < LargeMultiplier < OverLimit < MachineLimit < CannotRepresent
       ↑              ↑                ↑            ↑              ↑
     —          Readiness        Readiness    Containment   Representability

  So the rename and the class map together give you a readable level and an
  unambiguous axis — and admission_by_class() (landing now) exposes them
  separately.

  I'll take the rename now, before the remaining steps write more call sites
  under the old names, and wire MachineLimit conservatively — RssLimitExceeded
  only — with the ambiguous variants flagged in the code rather than guessed.
  Starting with the enum and the class map, since everything else follows from
  those.

❯ OverLimit should not lead to "try with a larger limit" but rather "try with   
  no limit (not for production) other than "don't crash the machine"            
  contianment, a different class of limit.                                      

● That's a real correction, and it changes the remedy text I already committed.

  You're saying: incrementing an arbitrary number from 3,000 to 10,000 is still
  arbitrary. The informative move is to remove the artificial cap entirely and
  let machine containment be the only limit — then you learn the truth: it fits,
  or it's a MachineLimit.

  I need to flag a conflict this creates, because a doc/code mismatch is exactly
  what this sweep exists to eliminate. The spec says the opposite in as many
  words (spec:79-80):

  ▎ The public route to more production headroom remains an explicit retry with 
  ▎ a larger named resource envelope, not this switch.

  Both can be true if we separate purpose: a bigger envelope is how you get a
  shippable artifact with more headroom; removing caps is how you learn what's 
  actually true. An OverLimit finding is a diagnostic statement, so its remedy
  should be the diagnostic one. But the spec needs updating to say that, or
  we've just created the next mismatch.

  A synthesis that satisfies you, the reviewer, and the spec's literal wording:
  make "no artificial limits" a named envelope — something like
  MachineLimitOnly. All internal deterministic caps off; watchdog, RSS ceiling,
  job-object cap, and the absolute chain-depth ceiling all still on.

  - Your requirement: the remedy is "no limit but don't crash the machine" ✓
  - Reviewer's requirement: selection takes an envelope id, never the cfg-gated
    CompileSizeMode, so candidacy can't differ between production and developer
    builds ✓
  - Spec's wording: it is an explicit retry with a named envelope ✓
  - And because d77bb1cf now records the envelope id in the manifest, anything
    built under it is identifiable and refusable for publication ✓

  That also redirects step H: rather than adding arbitrary bigger rungs (a 10k
  enumeration cap that'll be wrong for the next grammar), add one
  uncapped-but-contained envelope. Far less churn, and it's the thing that
  actually answers your original question for all five grammars.

  The remedy string I committed in 70415c4a currently reads "Re-run under a 
  larger named resource envelope… the developer stress switch is not a route to 
  a production artifact." Under your direction the first half is wrong — it
  should point at the uncapped-but-contained run. I'll fix that as part of the

  Two are straight analysis, one is a post-compile measurement, one is a
  containment abort. They're produced by different subsystems at different times
  — which is the real reason max() over them is meaningless. It isn't a scale
  at all.

  The load-bearing change is your Error rule. Today is_normal_candidate()
  requires worst_severity() <= Warning, so an Error excludes a backend from
  selection entirely — you never get an FST. Under your model the compile
  succeeds, the artifact gets labeled, and only publication is refused. That
  single change is what will let 4 of the 5 grammars actually produce something
  instead of reporting "no path."

  One thing you already have: the difference between Warning and Error is
  predicted-vs-measured, and ValueProvenance { Predicted, ProvenBound, Observed 
  } already encodes exactly that on every finding. A LargeMultiplier is
  Predicted; a 150 MB artifact is Observed. No new machinery needed for that
  distinction.

  Naming — I'd take your semantics with the meaningful names:
  LargeMultiplier · NotProductionReady · MachineLimit · CannotRepresent
  (NotProductionReady over OverLimit, because your own phrasing — "after 
  compiling, is it production ready?" — is more accurate than "over a limit,"
  and size is only one of several readiness measures.)

  4. Keep publication refusing it, so nothing unshippable ships

     rule supersedes

  Your sharpening exposes something I'd have missed. ResourceBudgetReached is
  currently used for both an artificial internal cap tripping and the host
  watchdog killing a child — worker.rs:850-931 maps every watchdog outcome to
  it. Under your split those are now different categories entirely: an
  artificial cap is analysis-adjacent policy, the watchdog is machine health.
  One code can't be both, so it has to split.

  There's also a category your three don't cover, and I want a recommendation
  rather than a guess: BackendCompilationFailed and BuildProcessFailed describe
  the tool failing — bad input, worker protocol violation, pangloss.exe not
  existing. That's not analysis, not size, and not machine health. Either the
  model needs a fourth slot, or those fold somewhere. I've asked for reasoning
  rather than letting an agent pick.

  And a constraint I flagged so minimality doesn't break compatibility:
  health.rs states FindingCode is "the immutable PGFdddd registry: codes never 
  renumber after publication." So retiring or merging codes needs a migration
  recipe — reserve the number, keep a deserialize alias, never reuse — not just
  deletion.

● Agent(Retire stale severity vocabulary in docs) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Two agents running; the rename is still sweeping call sites.

  What the consolidation analysis found

  It traced every producer of all 13 finding codes and the result is more
  consolidation than I expected — plus two genuine bugs I've now verified
  myself:

  Bug 1 — the two process codes are used swapped. missing() (backend binary
  unavailable) emits BackendCompilationFailed, whose doc says "failed while 
  compiling its emitted representation." failed() (a compile that failed) emits
  BuildProcessFailed, whose doc says "Invalid build input, worker protocol 
  failure." Each constructor emits the other's documented meaning. Their own
  contract test already gives up on distinguishing them — it asserts
  matches!(code, BackendCompilationFailed | BuildProcessFailed).

  Bug 2 — ApplicationTimeWork has zero producers. It exists only in the
  registry, with a doc comment admitting no instrumentation emits it.

  And the split your model forces: ResourceBudgetReached currently covers both
  an internal cap tripping and the host watchdog killing a child. Those are now
  different categories. The good news — they're cleanly separable today, because
  internal caps always emit at Error and every watchdog kill emits at Critical.
  Nothing mixes.

  One finding I want to flag as a real category leak, because it bears directly
  on "not production ready is all size": PayloadSizeBand is definitionally
  post-compile, but for a mid-band payload it currently passes through the
  LargeMultiplier rung — which is defined as pre-compile static analysis. So a
  size measurement can currently surface wearing an analysis verdict. That's the
  ordinal scale leaking across your categories, and no amount of code
  consolidation fixes it; it needs the size bands to map only onto size
  verdicts.

  Where it pushed back, correctly: it recommends keeping ResourceBudgetReached
  and ProvenBoundExceedsBudget separate — a proven bound stops you before you
  spend the compute, an observed trip stops you after. Different remedy, worth
  the distinction. And it recommends keeping the three fst-health telemetry
  codes as telemetry rather than deleting them to satisfy a model that was never
  about telemetry. Minimal, not lossy.

  Once the rename lands I'll implement the consolidation, then run your two
  review passes.

● The size-band leak turned out to be more clear-cut than I expected, because
  IDEAL_MAX_BYTES is already exactly 100 MB — the threshold you named. So the
  four bands (100 MB / 200 MB / 1 GB / 5 GB) collapse to the single rule you
  stated: at or under 100 MB is fine, over it is NotProductionReady.

  That kills the leak outright. No size input can produce an analysis verdict,
  because there's no longer a band that maps there.

  I gave the agent one firm constraint: do not change any threshold value. 100
  MB stays 100 MB. It's changing which verdict a band maps to, not where the
  band sits — those are very different risks, and the constants carry a lot of
  documented reasoning about why the exact numbers are provisional.

  The interesting part is the fallout: INFO_MAX_BYTES, WARNING_MAX_BYTES and
  ERROR_MAX_BYTES have 33 references between them. Some only fed the removed
  bands and should go; some are doc comments explaining a band structure that no
  longer exists; and if anything outside pg-foma consumes one, deleting it is a
  breaking public-API change. I asked for a decision per constant with 
  reasoning rather than a blanket removal — minimal shouldn't mean lossy.

  Also flagged size_band_crossed_threshold, which maps a severity back to the
  band edge it crossed and carries an unreachable!(). If size can no longer
  produce LargeMultiplier, that function must not still claim it as a size edge
  — otherwise the leak just moves.

  One item left after this: stopping NotProductionReady from excluding a backend
  from selection. Then your two review passes.

● Agent(Stop size from blocking selection) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● 9cf7f6e3 landed — twelve commits, tree clean, everything green.

  The final code change is running, and it's the one with real consequences:
  is_normal_candidate() currently excludes a backend when worst_severity()
  exceeds LargeMultiplier, so a grammar whose only problem is a 200 MB payload
  reports "no backend admitted" and never produces an FST at all. After this it
  will compile, and carry the label.

  The predicate becomes class-aware rather than ordinal: exclude when the
  backend cannot produce a usable artifact — CannotRepresent (nothing to build)
  or MachineLimit (the attempt was aborted) — and never merely for size. That's
  your rule expressed as code rather than as a threshold comparison.

  I specified three guardrails as required tests, because this is exactly where
  a mistake would let unproven output ship:

  1. validate_health_readiness must still refuse to write a .pgpack at
     NotProductionReady or worse — compiling and publishing are different gates
     and only the second one moves
  2. No CannotRepresent finding becomes selectable by any route, with any flag
  3. --allow-unproven semantics unchanged

  And I warned it that this flips behaviour existing tests pin — including one
  whose name encodes the old rule
  (tuned_surface_resource_finding_is_reported_and_error_is_not_selected). Its
  instruction: for each failure, say whether it pinned the old rule and gets
  replaced with one pinning the new rule, or whether it reveals a real problem
  and it should stop. Never weaken an assertion to reach green.

  After this: your two review passes — conformance first, then an independent
  Fable pass over the whole thirteen-commit result.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The predicate now reads as your model rather than as a threshold:

  self.status == BackendStatus::Accepted
      && !matches!(self.decision, CompileDecision::Refuse(_))
      && !self.findings.iter().any(|finding| {
          matches!(finding.code.class(),
                   FindingClass::Representability | FindingClass::Containment)
      })

  It asks which question failed, not how high on a scale. Representability means
  the feature can't be proposed; Containment means the attempt was stopped
  before producing anything. Both leave nothing to select. Readiness — including
  an oversized payload — no longer excludes anything, because that artifact
  exists.

  The old line it replaced was worst_severity() <= Severity::LargeMultiplier — a
  single ordinal comparison across three unrelated axes, which is the original
  defect in miniature.

  I checked one thing the brief didn't cover: FindingClass::Process isn't in the
  exclusion list, and a tool fault also means no artifact. It's safe, because
  status == BackendStatus::Accepted is still required and the only producers of
  Process findings (missing()/failed()) set non-Accepted status. But that's an
  implicit invariant holding the door shut — a test asserting "a Process finding
  never appears on an Accepted report" would pin it. That goes to the review
  pass rather than getting bolted on now.

● The two failures were the right kind — a test whose premise the change
  invalidated, caught rather than papered over.

  What was wrong with it: it asserted NotProductionReady and MachineLimit are
  both non-selectable, which is exactly the rule we replaced. Worse, it built an
  incoherent finding — Severity::MachineLimit paired with
  FindingCode::UnknownUnboundedConstruct, which is a Readiness-class code. Under
  the old ordinal predicate that passed, because only the severity number was
  consulted. Under a class-aware predicate the pair is contradictory: a
  machine-limit severity on a readiness-class code.

  That's a nice incidental result — the class-aware predicate surfaced an
  incoherent test fixture that the ordinal one couldn't see.

  I replaced it with two tests using coherent pairs:

  - an_oversized_payload_labels_a_backend_without_excluding_it — PayloadSizeBand
    + NotProductionReady is selected, and the label survives selection
  - containment_and_representability_findings_exclude_a_backend —
    HostContainmentFired + MachineLimit, and BackendCoverageIncomplete +
    CannotRepresent, neither selected but both retained in reports

  Plus the integration copy, which now asserts both halves in one place:
  readiness stays selectable, containment doesn't.

  That's stronger coverage than before — the old test could only say "these
  don't get selected"; the new ones say why, and would fail if someone
  reintroduced an ordinal comparison.

● Two agents running on disjoint files. Here's where the sweep stands after the

  Verdict: DONE-WITH-FOLLOW-UPS, no P0

  It confirmed the split is real, not cosmetic — the selection predicate asks
  "which question failed" rather than "how high on a scale," the codes are
  class-correct, and each change carries a behavioural pin. And it verified the
  thing that mattered most: no CannotRepresent or NotProductionReady artifact 
  can reach production publication. All three blocking verdicts sit above the
  Ord floor the gate uses, pinned in three tests.

  The finding that stings

  Your central rule was never wired. Every production caller of evaluate_health
  passes payload_bytes: None. severity_for_size_bytes fires on nothing real. We
  spent this sweep making "not production ready is all size" precise, collapsed
  four bands to one clean threshold, and pinned it with five tests — against a
  function no production path calls. A 500 MB pack would publish clean on the
  size axis.

  That's a pre-existing gap rather than something the sweep broke, but it's
  exactly the kind of thing that would have let us declare victory on a rule the
  system doesn't actually enforce. It's now the first fix in flight, with the
  end-to-end test the reviewer said couldn't exist yet.

  Also in flight

  CompileWorkBudget is classed Containment while its only producer is an
  approaching-not-tripped magnitude observation — so a healthy 80%-of-budget
  build would lose candidacy if that finding ever reached a BackendReport.
  Latent, untested, wrong-way. Plus NotProductionReady's doc claiming "measured
  AFTER a successful compile" when three producer families contradict it, and
  Process being added to the selection predicate to make an implicit invariant
  explicit.

  Its recommendations I accepted without change

  Keep admission() narrowly as the gate floor but display admission_by_class();
  leave pg-assess's enum alone with one doc line; keep ChildCrashed as
  containment since it fails conservatively. And it flagged something I'd have
  missed — the wasm binding now returns "not_production_ready" where it returned
  "error", so a JS gate comparing === "error" silently passes everything. Serde
  aliases cover reading old reports, not consumers of new output.

● Agent(Surface the per-axis verdict to users) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Taking the last two items rather than leaving them open, since "getting rid of
  misaligned work" is exactly what an API with zero consumers is.

  Item one — the per-axis view reaches nobody. We built admission_by_class()
  specifically because one collapsed severity can't distinguish "too big to
  ship" from "partially unrepresentable" — then every reporting surface kept
  printing only the max. So the refusal message an operator sees still says a
  band name rather than which axis failed. The gate logic stays untouched; only
  what it says gets richer. I made the regression guard explicit: the same set
  of reports must be refused before and after.

  Item two — a rename that can rot silently. fstHealthAdmission() now returns
  "not_production_ready" where it returned "error". A JS consumer written as if 
  (admission === "error") doesn't fail — it silently stops blocking anything.
  Serde aliases protect reading old reports; nothing protects consumers of new 
  output. The fix is a boolean binding that can't drift with a rename, with the
  string getter kept for compatibility and documented as having changed in
  schema 3.

  That second one is the same class of defect as everything else this sweep
  removed: a claim that was correct when written, with no mechanism to notice
  when it stopped being. The difference is that this one we introduced
  ourselves, and the reviewer caught it before it shipped.

  Once this lands, the sweep is closed and the five-grammar work is unblocked —
  now against a model that reports "it fits" versus "it crashes at 30 GB" as two
  different verdicts instead of one indistinguishable refusal.

  The finding your instinct was pointing at

  I verified this before writing anything else. Your "grinding to a halt" is
  structural, not incidental:

  is_normal_candidate() =
      status == Accepted
      && !Refuse
      && no finding of class Representability | Containment | Process

  And these are Containment:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Code           │                  What it is                   │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ HostContainmentFired     │ the watchdog — correctly blocks               │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ResourceBudgetReached    │ an internal artificial cap tripped            │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ProvenBoundExceedsBudget │ an arbitrary number says it would trip —      │
  │                          │ fires before construction is even attempted   │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So we correctly classified the four questions, but the artificial caps still
  sit in the class that excludes a backend entirely. ProvenBoundExceedsBudget is
  the worst of it: a proven-bound check refuses a build that was never
  attempted, on a constant whose own docs admit to being provisional. That is
  exactly "an arbitrary number stops us getting anything."

  Classification alone didn't unblock you. The defaults still refuse.

  Where we were, a day ago

  - Health had one ordered scale — Ideal/Info/Warning/Error/Critical — consumed
    by max(). Three unrelated questions collapsed into one number.
  - A 3,000-visit cap and a genuine "cannot represent infixation" produced the
    same is_no_path(). Indistinguishable downstream.
  - Four of five reference grammars reported "no path" with no way to tell why.
  - pg-foma's test suite did not compile — a red gate referenced nine fields
    that never existed, so all 1154 sibling tests were unrunnable, and every
    green result came from narrow -TestTarget runs.
  - Docs had been rewritten to the new model; code had not been touched.

  Where we are now — 17 commits

  - Four verdicts naming facts: LargeMultiplier, NotProductionReady,
    MachineLimit, CannotRepresent, plus FindingClass as the axis, exhaustive so
    a new code can't compile unclassified.
  - Selection routes on class, not ordinal. A size label no longer withholds a
    backend.
  - Size is one 100 MB threshold, wired to the real payload — it was previously
    enforced by nothing at all.
  - Machine health separated from internal caps; tool faults separated from
    both.
  - pg-foma compiles: 1162/1163. All five crates green, hygiene clean, only 5
    pre-existing failures (4 of which live on main).

  The FST compile pipeline, with every gate

   ┌─ FRONT END ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ grammar load (fwdata / hc.xml / snapshot)                           │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ GrammarSemantics::derive  →  CharacteristicsProfile                 │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ capability predicates, PER BACKEND                                  │
   │   → CompileDecision { Admit | ConfirmOnly | Refuse }                │
   │   ⛔ GATE 1  Refuse ──────────────────────► CannotRepresent          │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ characterization_findings                                            │
   │   ⚠  LargeMultiplier   (N×M×O too large — analysis)                  │
   │   ⛔ GATE 2  ProvenBoundExceedsBudget ◄── ARBITRARY CONSTANT,        │
   │                                            REFUSES BEFORE BUILDING  │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                  ↓
   ┌─ SELECTION ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ select_backends → BackendReport per backend                         │
   │   ⛔ GATE 3  is_normal_candidate:                                    │
   │        excludes Representability | Containment | Process            │
   │        (Readiness passes — a size label no longer blocks)           │
   │   → selected() / preferred() / is_no_path()                         │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                  ↓
   ┌─ BACK END (in a killable worker child) ─────────────────────────────┐
   │ emit → lexc source     ⛔ GATE 4 enumeration + compose budgets      │
   │                                  → ResourceBudgetReached           │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ foma compile → Fsm    ⛔ GATE 5 closure work / depth caps           │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ ╔═ WATCHDOG (parent process, non-disableable) ═══════════════╗      │
   │ ║ RSS ceiling · wall clock · procgov job-object memory       ║      │
   │ ║   ⛔ GATE 6 → HostContainmentFired / MachineLimit          ║      │
   │ ╚════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝      │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                  ↓
   ┌─ EVIDENCE & PUBLISH ────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ evaluate_health(payload_bytes, profile) → HealthReport               │
   │   ⚠  NotProductionReady  (payload > 100 MB — label only)            │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ select_completed_build  ⛔ GATE 7 envelope + grammar identity        │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ validate_health_readiness                                            │
   │   ⛔ GATE 8  refuses ≥ NotProductionReady → no .pgpack              │
   │        ↓                                                            │
   │ .pgpack written (manifest records envelope id + size mode)          │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                  ↓
                runtime: into_analyzer → propose → peel → confirm

  Eight gates. Only Gate 6 is real machine safety. Gates 2, 4 and 5 are
  artificial constants, and Gates 2–3 together are what turn "big and slow but
  works for a test" into nothing at all.

  What I think the answer is

  Your four categories are right and implemented. What's missing is that
  artificial caps should not live in a class that excludes. Two candidate fixes,
  and I'd want the reviews' verdict before choosing:

  1. Move internal caps out of Containment into a Readiness-like class so they
     label rather than exclude, leaving Containment to mean only the watchdog.
     Then only Gate 6 can deny you an artifact.
  2. Change the default envelope to caps-off for stress work, so Gates 2/4/5
     don't bind and the machine is the limit — which is what you asked for two
     messages ago ("try with no limit other than don't crash the machine").

  They compose: (1) is the classification fix, (2) is the policy fix. Together
  they'd mean a poorly-designed grammar yields an FST plus a stack of warnings,
  which is what you actually want for detecting warnings and improving from
  there.

  On churn and drift, honestly

  There was real churn: the naming went through four proposals before landing,
  and I made two sequencing errors (dispatching before committing, and
  committing on one crate's evidence). But every code change was verified
  against all five crates, and I've deliberately left four items open with 
  reasons rather than half-finishing them. The specific drift question — "is the
  new classification bolted onto an older model rather than replacing it" — is
  the headline question I gave the architecture reviewer, because the
  Containment-excludes-selection finding above suggests the answer may be partly
  yes.

● Agent "Architecture review" finished · 6m 45s

● The architecture review is in, and it answers your central worry directly.

  On drift: verified clean, and that's an evidence-based finding

  The reviewer looked hardest exactly where you were concerned and reports: "I 
  did not find half-migrated concepts, silently-duplicated mechanisms, or unused
  new abstractions from this specific 17-commit span." The evidence it cites:

  - Every renamed variant carries #[serde(alias)] with a test pinning that
    pre-schema-3 reports still parse — a real migration, not rename-and-hope
  - admission_is_unchanged_by_the_per_class_view proves the publish gate is
    bit-for-bit unchanged
  - admission_by_class is consumed — backend_selection.rs:248, pack.rs,
    make_report.rs, fst_health.rs — so it's not an orphaned abstraction
  - size_never_reports_an_analysis_verdict is "the kind of regression test you
    write when you actually understand what you broke"

  So the churn was noisy but not damaging. The real architectural problems are 
  older and larger than anything this sweep touched.

  The two that matter, and they're both structural

  1. Your IR is consumed by the wrong end of the compiler. plan.rs is a genuine
  content-addressed, deduplicating DAG — the most compiler-shaped thing in the
  codebase. But enumerate.rs:359-362 shows both shipping backends
  (TunedSurfaceProbed, TemplatedUnderlyingTokens) ignore Plan entirely and
  derive their own topology straight from &Grammar. Only PlanComposed —
  self-described as "an explicitly minimal prototype" — interprets it. Meanwhile
  capability.rs does walk Plan bottom-up.

  So the IR feeds the front-end capability check, not codegen. You don't have
  three code generators sharing an IR; you have three independent front-to-back 
  compilers reconciled after the fact by a hand-curated 3×22 table in
  strategy_coverage.rs. That's precisely why "which backend can represent X" has
  to be answered by a citation table instead of by asking the IR — and it's the
  root cause of the compounding-complexity problem you described.

  2. You already built the better answer and production doesn't use it.
  backend_registry + backend_mechanism + mechanism_provider express what a
  mechanism requires in CharacteristicKind terms and resolve per-strategy
  support by lookup — exactly the design where adding a backend is local. It's
  wired into recipe_optimize.rs only. Production backend_selection.rs still
  hardcodes the 3-variant enum across 162 references in 15 files. Every week
  that passes adds more sites to migrate.

  What it says to do before alpha

  Priority: 1
  Action: Redocument plan.rs as a capability-analysis DAG, not an IR — the
  current doc invites a fourth-backend author to assume IR-sharing they won't
  get
  Effort: an afternoon
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Priority: 2
  Action: Promote backend_mechanism to the production selection path
  Effort: large, but already-decided work
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Priority: 3
  Action: Split capability.rs (3,942 non-test lines, 15 predicates, one file)
  Effort: medium, mechanical
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Priority: 4
  Action: One HealthFinding builder — three modules hand-assemble a 10-field
  literal
  Effort: small
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Priority: 5
  Action: Sink the shared OverrideRecord shape into a leaf crate so the compiler

  enforces the mirror instead of a comment
  Effort: small-medium

  It also raises a scope question I'd put to you: if PlanComposed/uflexc stays
  permanently the weakest non-default backend with two whole-construct coverage
  holes, is it worth keeping as a third strategy at all — or folded away before
  it multiplies the migration surface in item 2?

  Clean-code and alignment+efficiency reviews still running; the Fable synthesis
  follows once both land, and I'll feed it this plus your alpha timeline so the

  And that same pre-check is pure waste

  tuned_surface_resource_finding_with_limit runs the production emitter's real 
  closure traversal and discards the result just to raise a finding — then
  FomaProposer::new runs the same traversal again for real. Every successful
  compile pays for the closure walk twice. Under --watchdog the parent pays for
  it too, and passes nothing across the boundary to the child.
