  All of this exists so you can choose between capped and uncapped builds. Your
  flow has only uncapped. Two "envelopes" that differ by one number, a retry
  system that permits retries no envelope can satisfy, and a whole compile-time
  feature flag all collapse to nothing. resource_envelope.rs (490 lines) mostly
  disappears.

  2. Every internal cap's power to stop a build. compose_budget.rs is 1,334
  lines and referenced 165 times across 27 files —
  state/arc/tuple/line/compound-pair/enumeration/closure caps. Keep the counters
  (they're your "FST node estimate"). Delete every comparison that refuses.
  Only exception: keep one absolute loop-guard so an infinite recursion still
  stops.

  3. ProvenBoundExceedsBudget — delete outright. 25 refs. This refuses to even 
  start a build by comparing a predicted count against 3,000 — a number whose
  own comment admits nothing was measured to pick it. This is the single biggest
  cause of "no FST for you." The count it computes becomes your node estimate
  instead.

  4. The refusal to write the output file. Right now a too-big-but-correct FST
  is never written to disk. Your flow: always write it, stamp the label.

  5. Backend ranking. BACKEND_PREFERENCE (12), rank_key (3), worst_severity (4).
  You said "right now we should be able to build any of them that can represent
  it" — so selection is a filter, not a ranking. This also deletes the last
  place a severity ordinal drives a decision.

  6. In-build work estimates. The proposal-volume / confirmation-work /
  duplicate-overlap calculations. You're right that analysis can't compute these
  — they belong in the separate word-test step.

  7. One of the two duplicate emission pipelines. emit.rs has two ~900-line
  functions that differ only by one already-parameterized flag, with comments
  admitting it. ~400 lines out.

  The one you flagged as top priority — and a conflict I have to name

  You're right that there are two systems. Here are the sizes:

  - Old, wired to production: backend_selection.rs — 1,140 lines
  - New, wired to nothing but the optimizer: backend_registry +
    backend_mechanism + mechanism_provider + backend_optimizer + backend_report
    + backend_runtime — 7,789 lines

  So the unwired system is 7× larger than the one actually running. And what it
  does is score and rank backends — the exact question you just said is deferred
  ("for production it matters, but right now we should be able to build any of
  them that can represent it").

  My recommendation: rip the new 7,789 lines, keep the small old one, and strip 
  its ranking down to a can-represent filter. That's ~8,900 lines gone and it
  matches your flow exactly.

  But I have to flag a conflict: there's a recorded decision from 2026-07-31
  that says the opposite — the optimizer becomes the only path and the old paths
  get stripped, once conformance plus four corpora beat the hand-built version.
  That precondition hasn't been met. The architecture review also recommended
  promoting the new system, because it's the cleaner way to add a backend later.

  I don't think I should silently reverse a recorded architectural decision on
  7,789 lines. Your call:

  - Rip the new one — matches your flow, ~8,900 lines gone, fastest path to
    alpha. Cost: when you add backend #4, you re-solve the "add a backend
    cleanly" problem.
  - Rip the old one — honors the 2026-07-31 decision, but it's a large migration
    into code production has never exercised, and it would likely cost you this
    week's alpha.

  One thing to add, not rip

  its own independent memory ceiling and dies on its own allocation — nothing

● Two things worth your time, in plain terms.

  Your own test suite already proves the point

  five_language_backend_reports_gate.rs pins four of your five grammars as "no 
  path" — Indonesian, Amharic, Aweti, Mbugwe. And in all four, the only thing
  blocking them is that single 3,000 number. The same test file shows Indonesian
  compiling completely cleanly, zero warnings, when that number is raised to
  10,000.

  So it isn't a judgement call. One uncalibrated constant is at least 3.3× too
  small for a real grammar, four of your five grammars are failing on it, and
  your test suite has been recording that as expected behaviour.

  --watchdog can never produce a file. Ever.

  This is new — none of the earlier reviews caught it. When you run with the
  watchdog on (the safe, contained mode), the code path always returns "no
  payload," which always adds a "not production ready" mark, which the write
  gate always refuses. The contained mode and getting an artifact are mutually 
  exclusive by construction. That's the exact inverse of what you asked for:
  "build anything, only kill it if the machine is about to die."

  I got one thing wrong

  I flagged the backend ranking as a live bug. It isn't. Backends carrying
  representability, containment or tooling findings are already filtered out
  before ranking, so the severity comparison only ever happens among size labels
  — where comparing magnitudes is legitimate. It's sound; it's just not pinned
  as sound. I've corrected the running agent to make that explicit rather than
  delete it.

  On the two backend systems

  The verdict is emphatic: do not migrate to the new one now. 162 references
  across 15 files, days before alpha, replacing the exact substrate every test
  just got pinned against — that is the churn you're worried about, and it would
  invalidate the unblocking work mid-flight. The direction was decided; the
  week wasn't.

  So the live question is narrower than I framed it: not "which do we migrate
  to" but "do we delete the 7,789 unwired lines now, or freeze them and delete
  later." Freezing costs nothing this week. I'd freeze, ship alpha, then delete
  or promote from evidence.

  What's actually happening now

  Running:
  - Internal caps stop excluding backends; "containment" comes to mean only the
    real machine watchdog
  - The 3,000 pre-check becomes an estimate with a warning, not a refusal — and
    its remedy stops saying "try a bigger number"
  - Ranking invariant made explicit

  Queued behind the two agents still in pg-cli:
  - The write gate refuses on what kind of problem it is, not on a severity
    number — which safely keeps the real protection (a failed compile still
    writes nothing, because that's a tooling fault, not a size label)
  - Size mode threaded into the three call sites that ignore it
  - The --watchdog-produces-nothing inversion

  Then: run all five grammars with caps off under the job object, so the machine
  is the only limit. That run is what finally gives you real numbers to replace
  all three guessed constants — and it's the first honest answer to "does it
  fit, or does it die at 30GB."

  Deferring per the verdict: recalibrating the constants (do it from that run's
  data), splitting the containment class, and the capability.rs split. All are
  cheap later and all are merge-conflict fuel right now.

● You're right, and I need to correct something I told you earlier.

  Yes — it's a diagnostic system, and it's better built than my framing implied

  Look at what a single finding actually carries in the code:

  - the metric and its measured value
  - the reference figure it was compared against
  - whether the value was predicted or actually measured
  - which specific rules caused it, ranked by contribution
  - a plain-English explanation
  - ranked remedies, each flagged with whether it would change your language's
    meaning, plus a safety caveat

  One of those caveats, verbatim in the code: "Don't make any change that would 
  make your language invalid!"

  That is not a gatekeeper. Nobody writes a ranked list of remedies with a
  linguistic-safety warning for a system whose answer is "no." That's a tuning
  advisor — exactly "I can represent this, but here are three ways to make it
  more efficient." The concept you thought you were building is the concept that
  got built.

  The bug is purely in the wiring. Three of those advisory findings were filed
  under the category that excludes a backend, and one gate refuses to write the
  output file based on a severity number. So an advisory system was given a veto
  it was never designed to have. That's it. That's the whole "thou shalt not
  pass" problem — and it's what the agents are fixing right now.

  Where I was wrong

  I told you there were "two backend systems, rip one, 7,789 lines." That
  conflated three different things, and the recommendation to delete them was
  too blunt. Properly separated:

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │         What          │ Lines  │  What it actually    │     Verdict     │
  │                       │        │         does         │                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │                       │        │ The diagnostic       │                 │
  │ health / findings /   │ ~3,000 │ advisor. Measures,   │ Keep. Un-gate   │
  │ remedies              │        │ explains, suggests   │ it.             │
  │                       │        │ fixes                │                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │                       │        │                      │ Keep, shrink to │
  │ backend_selection     │ 1,140  │ The filter that      │  "can           │
  │                       │        │ picks backends       │ represent?" +   │
  │                       │        │                      │ override        │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │                       │        │ A cleaner way to     │ Real duplicate  │
  │ backend_registry +    │ ~2,700 │ answer "can this     │ of the          │
  │ backend_mechanism     │        │ backend represent    │ capability half │
  │                       │        │ this construct"      │  above          │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ backend_optimizer +   │        │ Automatically        │ Not a duplicate │
  │ backend_runtime +     │ ~5,000 │ searches for a       │  at all — a     │
  │ backend_report        │        │ better way to        │ separate        │
  │                       │        │ compile the grammar  │ feature         │
  └───────────────────────┴────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  That last row is the important correction. It isn't redundant machinery — it's
  "try one of these 3 ways to make it more optimal," done automatically instead
  of as advice. That's plausibly something you want, and I shouldn't have
  lumped it in with a delete recommendation.

  So the genuine duplicate is smaller than I said: ~2,700 lines of
  capability-answering that exists twice. The optimizer stays.

  What's in flight now

  Fixing exactly the clear bugs, as you said:

  1. Internal caps stop excluding backends — "containment" comes to mean only
     the real machine watchdog
  2. The 3,000 pre-check becomes an estimate with a warning, and its advice
     stops saying "try a bigger number"
  3. The write gate refuses based on what kind of problem it is, not a severity
     number — so a too-big-but-correct FST gets written and labelled
  4. --watchdog no longer structurally guarantees zero output
  5. Duplicate emission pipeline merged

  And the corpus is now assembled

  All five grammars plus their word lists are in one place — Indonesian,
  Amharic, Aweti, Mbugwe, Sena. They'd been scattered across four different
  worktrees, which is why those four grammar tests have been sitting #[ignore]d
  with nothing to point at. The verification run can now actually exercise them
  instead of reporting green on an empty set.

● Agent(Move payload off the wire frame) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Two agents running. The headline from the one that just finished is a single
  test rename that captures the whole change:

  an_oversized_pack_is_refused_publication
          →  an_oversized_pack_still_publishes_with_a_label

  That's your model, in the code. A too-big-but-correct FST now gets delivered 
  and labelled instead of thrown away.

  Also landed:

  - The write gate refuses on category, not a severity number. A size label
    publishes; a tooling fault, a representability gap, or a real containment
    kill still refuses. The placeholder-pack protection survives intact, because
    a failed compile is a tooling fault, not a size label — that's the check
    that made the flip safe.
  - --watchdog can now produce an artifact. The reason it never could was
    concrete: the generic worker path's success type had no payload field at 
    all. It's been added and wired through. There's a separate pipeline that did
    carry bytes, but it demands proof of complete closure — so it would have
    refused exactly the partial/oversized cases you want shipped with a warning.
    Right call not to reuse it.
  - The Apply-phase check was using phase-plus-severity as a stand-in for
    category, which mislabelled size findings as containment failures. Now it
    tests containment directly.
  - Size mode reaches the three call sites that ignored it — 3,000 under
    managed, unlimited under stress, with no change to existing managed-mode
    behaviour.

  And one new limit, being fixed the right way

  Putting the payload on the wire exposed a 16 MiB cap on the worker's result 
  frame — against a 100 MB label threshold. So there was a band where a grammar
  compiles fine, the design says "ship it with a label," and a pipe limit
  silently eats it.

  I did not raise the number. A bounded result frame is genuinely correct — a
  parent must never do an unbounded read from a child it may need to kill. So
  instead the compiled network moves out of band: the child writes it to a file,
  the frame carries only the path and length, the parent verifies the length
  matches before packaging. The frame stays small and safe; artifact size is now
  bounded by disk, which is a real machine limit you've said you accept.

  That's the last arbitrary number in the build-to-artifact path.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 23s

● Agent "Merge duplicate emission pipelines" finished · 24m 10s

● The emit merge landed: −524 lines, both entry points byte-identical
  signatures, zero caller changes. And because I told it to diff mechanically
  rather than trust the review summary, it caught two things the review missed —
  one of which would have been a silent regression.

  The landmine it avoided

  The two pipelines check roots.is_empty() at different points relative to the
  classification loop. One checks immediately; the other checks after populating
  slot/group counts. So for a grammar with no roots, the two produce different 
  report contents. A naive merge picking either order would have silently
  changed one caller's output. It parameterized the ordering instead of
  guessing.

  The finding worth your attention

  The templated path runs a post-emission tag-reachability check — it recompiles
  and looks for a tag that was declared, had entries written, but never made it
  into the compiled network's alphabet. That's a real correctness bug detector.

  Your default shipping backend has no equivalent check at all.

  The agent correctly refused to make that call unilaterally: adding it to the
  production path costs a foma recompile with no measurement to justify it, and
  dropping it from the templated path loses a real detector. So it's preserved
  exactly as-is behind a flag, with the question left open.

  The question is worth answering: does the surface-probed path have the same
  latent tag-unreachability exposure and simply never got the check ported? If
  yes, that's a silent-wrong-output bug in the default path, which matters more
  than anything else on the cleanup list — it's a correctness issue, not a
  tidiness one.

  Where the whole effort stands

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                  │                                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Refusal gates                    │ 8 → 3 (all three survivors           │
  │                                  │ legitimate)                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Machine-health category          │ 2 internal counters + watchdog →     │
  │                                  │ watchdog only                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Diagnostic signals that label    │ 11                                   │
  │ rather than block                │                                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Oversized FST                    │ refused → delivered with a label     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ --watchdog                       │ structurally could not produce       │
  │                                  │ output → produces output             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤

● Confirmed, and item 4 turns out to be the fifth instance of the same bug:

  if candidate.adapter.interprets_plan() {
      if let Some(reason) = unbuildable_marker_reason(candidate) {
          return failed_evaluated_over(
              EmissionStrategy::PlanComposed,
              Certification::Unsupported { reason },
              0,                          // <-- zero work done

          );
      }
  }

  Returns Unsupported with zero work measured, before anything is attempted.
  That single early return explains all four failures: the [0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0]
  no-confirmation-work, the evidence count of 2 instead of 5, and candidate 5
  reporting unsupported when its actual problem was a resource bound it never
  got far enough to hit.

  So the original handoff's "four recipe-optimizer regressions" were never four
  separate bugs — they're one premature refusal, the same shape as the four I've
  already removed.

  One honest caveat before I fix it. unbuildable_markers means the plan-composed
  compiler genuinely cannot build those subtrees — that's a real
  representability limit, which under your model is a legitimate refusal. So the
  question is whether the check is firing correctly on the wrong candidate, or
  firing correctly but too early to let a more specific verdict emerge. The test
  wanting resource-breach says the more informative verdict is being masked.
  I'll have that investigated rather than assume, because deleting a legitimate
  can't-represent check would be exactly the wrong lesson to draw from four
  successful fixes.

  Holding the edit until the full test run finishes — I don't want to change
  source under a run I'm about to draw conclusions from. Then: item 4, plus the
  26 hygiene violations.

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  The category I should have seen sooner

  Since nobody uses PanGloss yet, all backward compatibility is dead weight.
  There's serde aliasing for pre-schema-3 spellings, a MultiplicityMismatch
  variant whose own doc says "no longer produced, kept for deserializing old
  reports", a Truncated field carrying skip_serializing_if purely to preserve a
  legacy wire shape, three mirrored OverrideRecord shapes, and schema-version
  machinery. That's all built to serve readers that have never existed.

  Ironically, part of it is mine — I had an agent add serde aliases and a
  migration test during the rename, protecting stored reports that nobody has.

  Where the missing five figures probably are

  The test suite. 2,493 tests written against a design that has now changed
  twice, and it's the one surface no review has examined. Some tests pin
  behaviour we deliberately removed; two were already found asserting
  combinations no code can produce. That's the review's primary target.

  I gave it one guardrail worth stating: a failing test isn't automatically dead
  weight. The earlier passes found real guards that only one of two code paths
  had — deleting those would be a correctness regression dressed as
  simplification. So it has to distinguish "wrong now" from "inconvenient".

  The verification run is still going in parallel; the review is read-only so
  they don't contend.

● You've identified the real failure mode: an agent that hits a failing test
  after removing a cap will "fix" it by restoring the behaviour, and every
  deletion turns back into a regression. The staging has to make deleting the
  test part of the same stage, explicitly.

  Let me check the verification state first, since the rip agent will need build
  slots.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Phase 1 improved: 2,316 of 2,319 passing, 3 failures (down from 6). And two of
  the three prove your point exactly:

  - the_accuracy_path_agrees_with_certification_while_doing_no_confirmation_work
  - every_distinct_plan_fully_confirms_or_refuses_markers_explicitly

  Both pin the blanket marker refusal we just removed. The first literally
  asserts no confirmation work happens — which was the bug. An agent left alone
  with these would "fix" them by restoring the refusal, undoing the fix while
  reporting green.

  The staging plan

  The ordering principle is outside-in: delete the consumer before the thing
  consumed, so each stage leaves the next item genuinely dead and trivially
  removable. And the binding rule — a test pinning doomed behaviour dies in the 
  same stage as the behaviour, never gets repaired.

  ┌───────┬──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Stage │                 What                 │      Why this order      │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0     │ Rules of engagement                  │ No behaviour preserved   │
  │       │                                      │ to keep a test green     │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │                                      │ Must be first, or later  │
  │ 1     │ Align the 3 known failures           │ stages can't distinguish │
  │       │                                      │  own breakage from       │
  │       │                                      │ existing red             │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │                                      │ Leaf work, zero          │
  │ 2     │ Backward compatibility               │ dependents, safest place │
  │       │                                      │  to prove the rule       │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │ CompileSizeMode,                     │                          │
  │ 3     │ --remove-size-limits,                │ The chooser goes first   │
  │       │ developer-tools feature              │                          │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │ ResourceEnvelope,                    │                          │
  │ 4     │ ResourceEnvelopeId,                  │ Now dead after stage 3   │
  │       │ RetryAuthorization                   │                          │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 5     │ compose_budget refusal paths (keep   │ Enforcement, now with    │
  │       │ counters)                            │ nothing to enforce       │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │ Third marker pre-refusal + in-build  │                          │
  │ 6     │ work estimates + the discarded       │                          │
  │       │ double traversal                     │                          │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │       │ Dead machinery:                      │                          │
  │ 7     │ FailClosed/RefusalWitness, dead pub  │                          │
  │       │ API, unused modules                  │                          │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 8     │ Remaining duplication                │                          │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 9     │ Docs, housekeeping, tracker update   │                          │
  └───────┴──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  Deliberately excluded and needing your word: the 2,717-line duplicate
  substrate (D2) and PlanComposed's fate (H4). And the correctness items (G1–G3)
  are separate work — they're fixes, not rips, and mixing them in is how a
  deletion pass turns into a regression pass.

● Agent(Staged rip-out of dead code) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Dispatched — one agent, sole writer, no file contention.

  The brief opens by quoting your own diagnosis back at it, because that's the
  thing most likely to go wrong:

  ▎ "Agents will prefer to do regressions rather than rip out this pre-alpha 
  ▎ software's unneeded code."

  followed by the concrete trap — remove a cap, a test asserting the cap fails,
  "fix" it by restoring the cap, report success. Then five binding rules, the
  key one being: deleting a test that pins deliberately-removed behaviour is 
  correct, and must be stated plainly each time, paired with its counterweight —
  a test guarding a real property survives, and if it can't tell which it's
  looking at, it keeps the test and says so. Without that second half, "delete
  aggressively" becomes its own kind of damage.

  It commits per stage. A 5,000-line deletion as one blob can't be reviewed or
  bisected, and your "we have the history and can recover stuff" only holds if
  the history has usable granularity.

  Two things still running alongside it: the corpus phase of the verification
  (the five grammars, finally being exercised) and the second-pass review
  hunting further deletion targets. Both read-only, so neither contends with the
  rip. The review's findings will land as follow-up stages for the same agent
  rather than a new one.

  Two decisions I need from you

  Both are gated out of the rip because I won't delete them on my own judgment:

  1. The 2,717-line duplicate capability substrate (backend_registry +
  backend_mechanism + mechanism_provider). It answers "can this backend
  represent this construct" — the same question production already answers — and
  is wired only to the optimizer. It's the single largest item on the list.
  Deleting it contradicts a recorded 2026-07-31 decision to migrate toward it,
  and Fable said emphatically don't migrate before alpha. So: delete now, or
  freeze and decide after alpha?

  2. PlanComposed/uflexc. Permanently your weakest backend, two whole-construct
  coverage holes, and the only consumer of plan.rs. Keeping it means every
  future backend migration carries it along. Fold it away, or keep?

  Say the word on either and I'll extend the same agent's scope rather than
  starting another.

  What I changed about the approach. The old brief was nine rigid stages with
  heavy prescription and a lot of hedging. The new one is shorter and
  front-loads the single thing that actually matters:

  ▎ When code and a test disagree, the code wins and the test gets deleted or 
  ▎ rewritten. State it plainly each time; do not apologise for it.

  with exactly one counterweight — a test guarding cannot represent, machine 
  dying, or a correctness invariant survives, and genuine uncertainty means keep
  it and say so. Everything else is ordering.

  I also stopped asking about the 2,717-line substrate. It's reachable from
  recipe_optimize.rs, so it isn't dead code — removing it is a product call, not
  a cleanup, and I shouldn't keep putting it in front of you as though it were
  blocking the rip. It's out of scope until you say otherwise, and I won't raise
  it again unprompted.

  That's roughly 165 lines and two whole concepts gone.

  Two things worth flagging about how this is going:

  The deletions cascade, exactly as the outside-in ordering predicted. Removing
  the override_record field made five tests meaningless — they existed solely to
  prove a legacy record doesn't change admission. With the field gone there's
  nothing to prove. I kept the two in that group that test real properties
  (empty report admits, worst severity wins) and renamed them, since their old
  names all began fst_health_override_policy_ for a policy that no longer
  exists.

  One correction to the rip list. I had listed "three mirrored OverrideRecord
  shapes" as a single item. That's wrong — they're two different things.
  pg_pack::trust::CapabilityOverrideRecord is live: it's constructed at three
  sites in format.rs and backs CapabilityTrust::Overridden, which is the
  --allow-unproven trust stamp. Only the pg_foma::health one is dead. Deleting
  all three would have removed a working feature, which is precisely the
  "aggressive deletion becomes its own damage" case.

  Still to finish on this item: the OverrideRecord struct itself, the field
  declaration, pack.rs:167's copy line, six is_none() assertions that can no
  longer fail, the golden-JSON fixture entry, and four module-doc sections about
  override semantics. Then I can compile.

  I'll keep going and check in when I have something compiled rather than
  narrating each edit.
